## _**Issue 8 – Relative treatment effect**_ 

|**Questions for engagement**|a) Is it appropriate to only capture placebo response in current clinical management arm for 1 cycle<br>only (the length of the trial), or should the relative efficacy of CBD compared with current clinical<br>management remain constant over time?|
|---|---|
|**Background/description of issue**|A relatively large placebo response was observed across the trials included in the company<br>submission.<br>**The company**explained that large placebo effect is common in epilepsy trials and has been<br>observed in LGS studies since the 1990s. According to the company, the exact reason is unknown<br>but could be attributed to a number of reasons including the psychological expectation of<br>improvement and regression to the mean. To account for this background effect, the company<br>implemented a treatment response for current clinical management in the first cycle of the model<br>and assumed that this effect will be lost, with return to baseline occurring after the first cycle.<br>**The ERG**agreed that the large placebo effect was in line with that observed in trials of other anti-<br>epileptic drugs. However, it was concerned that the placebo effect was assumed to affect the<br>current clinical management cohort, for only the first cycle and that the clinical effectiveness of CBD<br>in subsequent cycles may be overestimated.<br>**The technical team**notes that there is no comparative data beyond 14 weeks (i.e. the first cycle of<br>the model) and that assuming the placebo effect is maintained in subsequent cycles may<br>overestimate the treatment effect of current clinical management.|
|**Why this issue is important**|Assuming the placebo effect for current clinical management persists only for 1 cycle might result in<br>an overestimated treatment effect for CBD.|
|**Technical team preliminary**<br>**judgement and rationale**|The technical team considers that assuming the relative efficacy of CBD compared with current<br>clinical management is constant over time may more closely reflect the benefits of CBD in clinical<br>practice.|

In other LGS trials, it has varied from a 5%<br>worsening to a 12% improvement.<br>The absolute impact of CBD in LGS on drop seizures from baseline is consistent across studies at<br>40-50%.<br>This magnitude of effect was observed in the open-label GWPCARE5 study and in the US Early<br>Access Program.<br>These observations suggest that the absolute reduction in seizure frequency in the clinical trials<br>would be replicated in clinical practice.<br>If the relative treatment effect were maintained throughout the time horizon (as preferred by the<br>ERG), CBD will be unduly penalised by virtue of the unusually high placebo effect.<br>The company provided 2 analyses:<br>1. |**Background/description of issue**|**The company**used efficacy inputs from GWPCARE 5 for months 3 to 27 in the model (CS, p66–<br>69). Treatment benefit was maintained for CBD from 27 months (see issue 10).<br>**The ERG**noted that the clinical inputs were based on evidence from a different dose of CBD than<br>included in the model and that the clinical benefit of CBD may therefore have been overestimated. It<br>therefore explored scenarios (ERG report, p102–104) where:<br>•<br>The costs of CBD were set to the 20 mg/kg/day dose after the first cycle, or<br>•<br>The clinical effectiveness of CBD was based on the 10 mg/kg/day dose only.|
|**Why this issue is important**|If the results from the open label study are not generalisable to the dose of CBD used in clinical<br>practice then the clinical benefit of CBD will be overestimated and the ICER underestimated.|
|**Technical team preliminary**<br>**judgement and rationale**|The results from GWPCARE 5 may not be generalisable to the use of CBD in clinical practice. The<br>ERG’s first scenario is likely to overestimate the ICER as increasing the dose of CBD in all patients<br>to 20 mg/kg/day is not likely to reflect clinical practice. The ERG’s second scenario is more plausible<br>but includes the assumption that treatment benefit continues throughout the model (see issue 10)<br>and does not account for dose escalation (see issue 11) or stopping rules (see issue 5).|

## _**Issue 10 – Extrapolating the effects of treatment beyond the follow up period in the clinical trials**_ 

|**Questions for engagement**|a) Should the model account for a potential decrease in treatment effect on drop seizure- and total<br>seizure frequency over time? If so, how should this be estimated? For example, are seizures<br>likely to return to baseline levels, and over what period – 2 years, 4 years or something else?<br>b) If the dose of other anti-epileptic drugs had been reduced (see issue 17) would the dose be<br>increased back to standard levels if the efficacy of CBD was reduced?|
|---|---|
|**Background/description of issue**|**The company**assumed in their model that after 27 months (maximum follow-up period of the open-<br>label extension study GWEPCARE 5) patients would remain in the same health state until<br>discontinuation or death (CS, p66).<br>**The ERG**noted that there was no evidence to support this assumption and presented two base-<br>case analyses, one using the company’s assumption and one assuming no treatment effect after 27<br>months (ERG report, p105-107).|
|**Why this issue is important**|If the treatment effect is not maintained over time, then the health gains associated with CBD would<br>be lower. In addition, there may be a need to increase the dose of other anti-epileptic drugs (if such<br>discontinuations have occurred), increasing the costs associated with the CBD plus current clinical<br>management treatment strategy. Both of these would worsen the cost-effectiveness estimates.|
|**Technical team preliminary**<br>**judgement and rationale**|The treatment effect of CBD may decrease over time. The company’s assumption therefore may<br>underestimate the ICER. The ERG’s assumption is likely to overestimate the ICER as they assume<br>people continue to take CBD, which would be unlikely as if there is no clinical benefit people would<br>stop CBD and costs would be expected to decrease. In addition, the treatment effect is more likely<br>to gradually diminish over time than abruptly stop at 2 years.|
|**Summary of comments**|**Comments received from clinicians**<br>The possibility of a reduction in treatment effect of CBD should be taken into account in the model.<br>Return to baseline levels on the same drug (combination) should be apparent within a year.<br>If the dose of any other anti-epileptic drugs had been decreased, it would likely be increased back to<br>standard levels if a reduction in treatment effect of CBD was observed.<br>**Comments received from company**<br>The treatment effect of CBD is unlikely to stop abruptly at any given time point.|

The company anticipates that most patients will be on this dose in clinical practice.<br>Of the groups described in Issue 11:<br>•<br>People who did not respond to a 10 mg/kg/day dose of CBD should not be considered for a<br>higher dose because there was no dose response in the CBD clinical trials.<br>•<br>People who are not responding to a 10 mg/kg/day dose of CBD should not be considered for<br>a higher dose.<br>•<br>People who responded to a 10 mg/kg/day dose have the option of being considered for a<br>higher dose of CBD in order to try to further reduce seizure frequency or possibly achieve<br>seizure freedom.<br>The company has implemented scenario analyses in a population that includes some patients who<br>receive a dose above 10 mg/kg/day, includingboth the costs and benefits.|
|**Technical team judgement after**<br>**engagement**|The company and clinical experts both indicated that some people who respond to 10 mg/kg/day of<br>CBD may have an increase in dose. The clinical benefits of this are likely to be captured in the<br>model if data from the open label extension study are used because some people in the extension<br>study had a dose increase. The technical team prefers that the cost of this is captured but notes that<br>the method used to calculate the average dose in the company’s scenario analysis may not reflect<br>the population who would have a dose increase in clinical practice.|

## _**Issue 12 – Time horizon**_ 

|**_Issue 12 – Time horizon_**||
|---|---|
|**Questions for engagement**|a) Are all differences in costs and effects attributable to CBD likely to be captured in a 15-year time<br>horizon?|
|**Background/description of issue**|NICE guide to the methods of technology appraisal stipulates that a lifetime time horizon is required<br>when alternative technologies lead to differences in survival, health benefits or costs that persist for<br>the remainder of a person's life.<br>**The company**used a time horizon of 15 years in its analysis and stated that this has been chosen<br>given the lack of long-term data.<br>**The ERG**considers this to be inconsistent with NICE methods, given the survival differences in<br>mortality that were attributed to CBD treatment. A 20-year time horizon was used by the ERG in<br>their base-case analysis as this is the maximum allowed in the company’s model.|
|**Why this issue is important**|People with LGS are at risk of higher mortality depending on their seizure frequency. Given the<br>potential effect of CBD on survival, The NICE methods guide suggests that a lifetime time horizon<br>should be used to accurately capture all the differences in costs and effects.|
|**Technical team preliminary**<br>**judgement and rationale**|A lifetime time horizon is required to accurately capture the incremental costs and benefits. This is<br>due to the survival benefit attributed to CBD in the model.|
|**Summary of comments**|**Comments received from clinicians**<br>A 15 year time horizon is insufficient to capture all costs and benefits. If effective, CBD is likely to be<br>continued, which may increase actual costs.<br>**Comments received from company**<br>The company’s updated base-case extends the time horizon to 50 years.<br>The company considers that a lifetime horizon in this therapy area should be based on the time<br>required for most patients to discontinue therapy. In theupdatedbase-case, only **** of patients<br>are still on therapy at 50 years. This is considered to be a reasonable lifetime horizon. Scenario<br>analyses with time horizons of 15, 20, 30 and 40 years are provided.<br>**Comments received from the ERG**<br>The ERGprefers a lifetime time horizon.|



Technical report – Cannabidiol for treating Lennox-Gastaut syndrome       Page 31 of 49 Issue date: July 2019 




<!-- page 409 -->

**Technical report template 2 – AFTER technical engagement** 

|**Technical team judgement after**<br>**engagement**|The technical team notes that **** of patients are still alive in each arm after 50 years and therefore<br>would have preferred a lifetime time horizon to fully capture costs and benefits. Because changing<br>the time horizon from 40 to 50 years did not substantially change the ICER the technical team<br>considers a 50-year time horizon can be used for decision making, although a lifetime time horizon<br>would be more appropriate.|

|**Summary of comments**|**Comments received from clinicians**<br>For adults, typically 2 carers attend with the patient in clinic.<br>**Comments from company**<br>No studies providing caregiver utilities in LGS have been identified from the literature. Carer quality<br>of life values are in line with those found in the literature for DS. A survey assessing caregiver<br>utilities using EQ-5D-VAS (Campbell et al., 2018) provided disutility values (0.33 +/- 0.21) that is at<br>the midpoint of those measured by the company’s vignette study. (0.27 and 0.40 for the two health<br>states with the highest numbers of seizures), validating the plausibility of the company’s disutility<br>values. A scenario using the disutility score from Campbell et al shows a similar ICER to the<br>company’s updated base case.<br>The literature indicates that people with severe epilepsy usually have more than 1 carer. In the large<br>pan-European DISCUSS survey of DS patients (Lagae et al., 2017), almost 80% of households had<br>more than one adult caregiver.<br>For many children with LGS, the need for multiple carers remains the same after they reach<br>adulthood because of cognitive and functional impairment. The company’s updated base case, in<br>line with Lagae et al, has assumed that each patient with LGS has 1.8 carers.<br>**Comments received from the ERG**<br>The plausibility of the estimated disutilities for caregivers is questionable. For instance, the<br>decrements for caregivers are more than 3 times as large as the decrements for patients.<br>If the carer disutilities are multiplied by 1.8 (assuming that each patient with LGS has 1.8 carers) as<br>done by the company, this would result in decrements for caregivers that are 5.5 to 11.7 times as<br>large than the decrements for patients.<br>The decrements provided by the company are based on the difference between the VAS-rated utility<br>and perfect health (i.e., utility of 1). The average utility in the population is lower than 1, so the<br>disutility for providing care based on Campbell et al. 2018 is likely to be overestimated.<br>Also, if multiple carers are involved, it is uncertain whether utility decrements should be on an<br>additive scale (e.g. not everyone in a family will have the same disutility)|
|---|---|
|**Technical team judgement after**<br>**engagement**|The scenario analysis provided by the company using alternative utility values from the literature<br>overestimates the quality of life reduction for carers. Assuming that people with LGS require 1.8<br>carers is plausible and in line with evidence.|

## _**Issue 16 – Impact of adverse events on quality of life**_ 

|**Questions for engagement**|a) Would the adverse events (AEs) associated with CBD be expected to have a substantial negative<br>impact on health-related quality of life?|
|---|---|
|**Background/description of issue**|Data from the included phase III trials of CBD, GWPCARE3 and GWPCARE4, show a pattern of<br>gastrointestinal and ‘tiredness’-related AEs in patients taking CBD, as well as some detrimental<br>effects on markers of liver function.<br>**The company**included costs related to these AEs in its model but did not account for its possible<br>negative impact on health-related quality of life.<br>**The ERG**questions this and considers including this negative impact on the quality of life of people<br>treated with CBD to be important. Given that the costs of these AEs were included in the model, it is<br>appropriate to also include the loss in quality of life that is likely to be associated with these events.<br>The ERG noted it was not feasible to implement these values in the model due to time constraints.|
|**Why this issue is important**|Ignoring the negative impact of treatment-related AEs on quality of life could result in overestimating<br>the QALY gain achieved for the CBD cohort. This might bias the results of the cost effectiveness<br>analysis in favour of CBD.|
|**Technical team preliminary**<br>**judgement and rationale**|The AEs associated with CBD are likely to have a negative impact on quality of life. This should be<br>accounted for in the model by including disutilities for these events.|
|**Summary of comments**|**Comments received from clinicians**<br>Adverse event potentially have a substantial negative impact on health-related quality of life, in the<br>context of multiple therapies and comorbidities.<br>**Comments received from company**<br>Most AEs associated with CBD reported in the clinical trials were mild to moderate in severity.<br>Therefore, any negative impact on health-related quality of life is likely to be very small compared to<br>the loss of quality of life associated with the severe seizures experienced by patients with LGS.<br>In addition, any AEs are occurring against a background of AEs from the other anti-epileptic drugs in<br>the CCM mix. Therefore, the costs associated with AEs have been included in the model, but the<br>disutilities that maybe associated with anyAEs have not.|



Technical report – Cannabidiol for treating Lennox-Gastaut syndrome       Page 41 of 49 Issue date: July 2019 




<!-- page 419 -->

|**Literature review**|The ERG identified errors in the company’s search strategy but the company did not include<br>the revised search strategy in the clarification response. |**Cost of current clinical management**|The ERG highlighted that the cost of ketogenic diet and vagus nerve stimulations was not<br>included in the model. Underestimation of this cost may bias the results, given the longer<br>survival of people treated with CBD. The technical team considers that these omissions are<br>not likely to substantially change the cost-effectiveness estimates.|
|**Cost of health states**|Resource use, and hence costs, for the “seizure-free” health state were considered to be<br>underestimated as it is not completely seizure-free. Additionally, the cost associated with<br>monitoring was not included. However, this is not anticipated to have substantial impact on<br>the model results.|
|**Institutionalisation rates**|Based on comments from the ERG the company updated institutionalisation rates in the<br>model to assume that people in the convulsive seizure-free state could be institutionalised.<br>The proportion was set to 2% based on clinical expert opinion.|
|**Body weight**|The ERG considered the use of median rather than mean body weight to be inappropriate.<br>Hence, the mean weight was used in the ERG base-case analysis.|
|**Dose titration period**|The titration period in the clinical trials is not included in the model, which uses the<br>maintenance dose of CBD from day 1. However, the ERG agreed with company that this is<br>likely to slightly overestimate treatment costs and have little effect on cost-effectiveness<br>results.|
|**Discontinuation rates**|The ERG considered that the discontinuation rates used by the company after cycle 1 were<br>not informed by evidence and lacked face validity (ERG report, p77). The technical team|



Technical report – Cannabidiol for treating Lennox-Gastaut syndrome       Page 47 of 49 Issue date: July 2019 




<!-- page 425 -->

**Technical report template 2 – AFTER technical engagement** 

||preferred the discontinuation rates used by the ERG. These were subsequently included in<br>the company’s updated base-case analysis.|
|---|---|
|**Quality of life value for the seizure-free**<br>**state**|The ERG commented on the quality of life value used by the company for the drop seizure<br>free health state(****)as it appears to be relatively high, given the likelihood of having non-<br>drop seizures. The ERG suggested that it is possible that patients may have misinterpreted<br>the vignettes. Based on these concerns, the ERG adjusted the quality of life estimate for the<br>health state “****************” (*****)in line with the quality of life value that is used in the Dravet<br>syndrome submission(*****)in the ERG base-case analysis.|
|**Parameter uncertainty**|Not all parameters have been included in the probabilistic sensitivity analysis (e.g. non-<br>sudden unexpected death in epilepsy costs). Following response to clarifications, the ERG<br>believes that the probabilistic sensitivity analysis still does not include all relevant parameters<br>(e.g. excluding discontinuation probabilities up to cycle 9, which are potentially influential).<br>The company reported that the PSA included all parameters that had a significant impact on<br>the ICER in the Deterministic Sensitivity Analysis (DSA).<br>The PSA for the Company’s Updated Base Case now includes the parameters of<br>“Subsequent discontinuation rates” (i.e. for cycles 2-9), non-SUDEP probability, and the<br>updated continuation/stopping criteria from NHS England (see Table 3).|
|**Innovation**|The company considers the drug to be innovative. However, clinical experts advise that it will<br>be an addition to the currently available anti-epileptic drugs and unlikely to represent a step<br>change in treatment since no patient in any of the included trials achieved complete freedom<br>from seizures.|
|**Equality considerations**|Comments from stakeholders during scoping noted that there was often difficulty in<br>accessing treatment as an adult, particularly where drugs were not licensed for adults –<br>despite there being no difference in the condition. The expected marketing authorisation for<br>CBD is likely to recommend it for use in people aged 2 years or older. When making<br>recommendations, the committee will consider whether any of them make it more difficult in<br>practice for a specific group to access the technology compared with other groups.|
